The sign-score and spectral-persistence principles have the same roles as above, but the dimensional determinant relation and its correction are proved afresh.

<a id="sec:envelopes"></a>
##### Background modes and strict envelopes

Let $$M=S^2\times\mathbb T^2,\qquad
 g_0=dp^2+\sin^2p\,d\theta^2+dz^2+d{z'}^2,$$ where the sphere has radius one and both circles have length $2\pi$. Fix a coordinate cube $P$ whose closure lies in $0<p<\pi/2$, and a smaller coordinate cube $D\Subset P$. Throughout the proof, integration against $dx$ in this chart means coordinate Lebesgue measure.

<a id="stmt-env-background-mode"></a>
<div id="env:background-mode" class="lemma">

<a id="env:background-gradient"></a>
**Lemma 25** (Background modes). *For every integer $k\ge1$, set $$\Lambda_k=2k^2+k,\qquad
 u_k^0=\sin^kp\cos(k\phi),\qquad \phi=\theta+z.$$ Then $u_k^0$ is a smooth real eigenfunction on $M$ with $(\Delta_{g_0}+\Lambda_k)u_k^0=0$. Set $f_0=\log\sin p$ on a neighborhood of $\overline P$. There is a constant $c>0$, independent of $k$, such that $$\label{env:background-gradient}
 |du_k^0|_{g_0}\ge ck e^{kf_0}\quad\hbox{on }\overline P.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-env-background-mode"><summary>Proof of stmt-env-background-mode</summary>

<a id="env:background-gradient-identity"></a>
*Proof.* In Cartesian coordinates on the sphere, $u_k^0$ is the real part of $(X_1+iX_2)^ke^{ikz}$. The polynomial $(X_1+iX_2)^k$ is homogeneous and harmonic, so its restriction to the unit sphere has eigenvalue $k(k+1)$; the $z$ factor contributes $k^2$. This proves smoothness and the eigenfunction equation. The covectors $df_0$ and $d\phi$ are orthogonal, with squared norms $\cot^2p$ and $\csc^2p+1$. Hence $$\begin{aligned}
 |du_k^0|_{g_0}^2
 &=k^2e^{2kf_0}\bigl(\cot^2p\cos^2(k\phi)
                  +(\csc^2p+1)\sin^2(k\phi)\bigr)\notag\\
 &=k^2\bigl(\sin^{2k}p+\sin^{2k-2}p-2(u_k^0)^2\bigr).
 \label{env:background-gradient-identity}
\end{aligned}$$ The first line gives <a href="#env:background-gradient" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="env:background-gradient">[env:background-gradient]</a>, since both coefficients have positive lower bounds on $\overline P$. ◻

</details>

<a id="env:condition"></a>
For a smooth function $f$ and a smooth metric $h$, write $$a=\nabla_h f,\qquad H=\nabla_h^2f,\qquad
 B=\sqrt{2+|a|_h^2}.$$ We call $f$ a *strict envelope* for $h$ on a set if $a\ne0$ there and $$\label{env:condition}
 H(a,a)+(2+|a|_h^2)
       \max_{\substack{|e|_h=1\\df(e)=0}}H(e,e)>0.$$ The maximum exists because the transverse unit sphere is compact. The condition is open in the second derivatives of $f$ and the first derivatives of $h$, as long as $df\ne0$.

<a id="env:metric-comparison"></a>
The function $f_0$ is a strict envelope for $g_0$ on $\overline P$. Indeed, with $e_\theta=(\sin p)^{-1}\partial_\theta$, $$a_0=\cot p\,\partial_p,\qquad
 H_0(\partial_p,\partial_p)=-\csc^2p,\qquad
 H_0(e_\theta,e_\theta)=\cot^2p,$$ and the expression in <a href="#env:condition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="env:condition">[env:condition]</a>, tested with $e_\theta$, is $$-\cot^2p\,\csc^2p+(2+\cot^2p)\cot^2p=\cot^2p>0.$$ Fix a smooth-topology open neighborhood $\mathcal U$ of $g_0$, sufficiently small in $C^1$ that $f_0$ remains strict for every $h\in\mathcal U$ on $\overline P$. Also arrange uniform comparison of these metrics with $g_0$ on $M$ and, for one fixed $K\ge1$, $$\label{env:metric-comparison}
 K^{-1}I\le (h_{ij}(x))\le KI
       \quad (x\in\overline P,\ h\in\mathcal U).$$ The finite-stage backgrounds used below satisfy the additional condition that $h-g_0$ has compact support in $P$.

<a id="pm-h-28"></a>
###### Amplifying the gradient

We reserve one direction for the old gradient and one transverse direction for the maximizing Hessian test in <a href="#env:condition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="env:condition">[env:condition]</a>. Corrugation in the remaining two directions supplies a large positive tangential Hessian that compensates for its negative radial Hessian. When this tangential term does not dominate, the reserved transverse direction retains the original strictness.

<a id="stmt-env-amplify"></a>
<div id="env:amplify" class="lemma">

<a id="env:amplification-gain"></a>
**Lemma 26** (Uniform amplification). *There is $d_*>0$, depending only on the comparison constant $K$ and fixed auxiliary functions, with the following property. Suppose $h$ is smooth, satisfies <a href="#env:metric-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="env:metric-comparison">[env:metric-comparison]</a> near $\overline D$, and $f$ is a strict envelope for $h$ on a neighborhood of $\overline D$. For every $b>0$ there is a smooth strict envelope $\widetilde f$ such that <a id="env:amplification-smallness"></a>$$\begin{gathered}
 \operatorname{supp}(\widetilde f-f)\Subset D,\qquad
 \|\widetilde f-f\|_{L^\infty}<b,\label{env:amplification-smallness}\\
 \int_D|\nabla_h\widetilde f|_h\,dx
 \ge(1+d_*)\int_D|\nabla_h f|_h\,dx.
 \label{env:amplification-gain}
\end{gathered}$$ Consequently the gradient integral can be made arbitrarily large by a perturbation compactly supported in $D$, with any prescribed positive total sup-norm budget. No derivative bound on the resulting envelope is asserted.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-env-amplify"><summary>Proof of stmt-env-amplify</summary>

<a id="env:radial-bowl"></a>
<a id="env:fixed-delta"></a>
*Proof.* Fix $0<r_0<r_1<R<1/2$ and a nonzero nonnegative function $s\in C_c^\infty((r_0,r_1))$. In the disk of radius $R$ about each point of $\mathbb Z^2$, define $$q(r)=-\int_r^R s(t)\,dt,$$ and set $q=0$ off the disks. The resulting function on $\mathbb R^2$ is smooth and $\mathbb Z^2$-periodic: it is a negative constant near each disk center and zero near each disk boundary. Where its derivatives are nonzero, let $n,t$ be the radial and tangential Euclidean unit vectors. Then $$\label{env:radial-bowl}
 Dq=s\,n^\flat,\qquad
 D^2q=s'\,n^\flat\otimes n^\flat
             +\frac{s}{r}\,t^\flat\otimes t^\flat.$$ Outside those annuli all derivatives in <a href="#env:radial-bowl" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="env:radial-bowl">[env:radial-bowl]</a> vanish. Fix also $0\le\chi\in C_c^\infty((-1,1)^4)$, equal to one on a smaller concentric box. Nonnegativity and bounded second derivatives give <a id="env:bump-estimates"></a>$$\label{env:bump-estimates}
 |s'|\le C\sqrt{s},\qquad |D\chi|\le C\sqrt{\chi}.$$ For example, for a nonnegative one-variable function $v$ with $\|v''\|_\infty\le L$, Taylor’s inequality at displacement $-v'/L$ gives $|v'|^2\le2Lv$; applying this along lines proves the second estimate as well. Let $$K_q=\sup s(s')_-,\qquad (s')_-=\max\{-s',0\},$$ and fix once and for all $0<\delta\le1$ so small that $$\label{env:fixed-delta}
 2K_q\delta^2\le\frac1{8R}.$$ This choice is independent of $f$, $h$, and all subsequent amplifications.

<a id="pm-h-29"></a>
###### The perturbation and its errors.

Subdivide $D$ into finitely many congruent coordinate cubes $C$ of side $L$. At the center $x_C$ of each cube choose constant linear coordinates $y$ centered at $x_C$ such that $h(x_C)=I$, $$a(x_C)=A_Ce_1,$$ and $e_2$ realizes the transverse maximum in <a href="#env:condition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="env:condition">[env:condition]</a> at $x_C$. Complete these to an $h(x_C)$-orthonormal frame. By <a href="#env:metric-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="env:metric-comparison">[env:metric-comparison]</a>, one fixed $c>0$ ensures that the $y$-box $|y_j|<\eta$, with $\eta=cL$, lies strictly inside $C$; its coordinate volume is a fixed positive fraction of $|C|$, uniformly over all cells. These frames need not vary continuously between cells.

<a id="env:corrugation"></a>
<a id="env:gradient-expansion"></a>
<a id="env:error-raw"></a>
Put $\epsilon=\eta^3$ and, in this box, add $$\label{env:corrugation}
 v_C(y)=\delta A_C\epsilon\,
       \chi(y/\eta)q\bigl((y_3,y_4)/\epsilon\bigr).$$ Extend $v_C$ by zero and set $\widetilde f=f+\sum_Cv_C$. The sum is finite, its terms have disjoint supports, and its support is compact in $D$. All constants in the error estimates that follow may depend on the current fixed $f,h$, but not on the cell or its fineness. In particular, $A_C$ has a positive lower bound and a finite upper bound. For brevity write $A=A_C$ in a given cell. Uniformly there, $h=I+O(\eta)$ and <a id="env:hessian-expansion"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 d\widetilde f
 &=A\bigl(e_1^\flat+\delta\chi s\,n^\flat+O(\eta)\bigr),
 \notag\\
 a_*:=\nabla_h\widetilde f
 &=A\bigl(e_1+\delta\chi s\,n+O(\eta)\bigr),
 \label{env:gradient-expansion}\\
 H_*:=\nabla_h^2\widetilde f
 &=H+\delta A(Q+\mathcal E),\qquad
 Q=\frac{\chi}{\epsilon}D^2q.
 \label{env:hessian-expansion}
\end{aligned}$$ Here $\chi$ and $q$ are evaluated at their arguments in <a href="#env:corrugation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="env:corrugation">[env:corrugation]</a>; $n,t$ lie in the $(y_3,y_4)$-plane. The differential of the cutoff term is $O(\epsilon/\eta)$. Two differentiations of <a href="#env:corrugation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="env:corrugation">[env:corrugation]</a>, including the connection term in the Hessian, give $$\label{env:error-raw}
 |\mathcal E|\le C\left(
  \frac{\sqrt\chi\,s}{\eta}+\frac{\epsilon}{\eta^2}
              +\chi s+\frac{\epsilon}{\eta}\right).$$ The second term includes the constant central part of the negative bowl. In particular it cannot be omitted when $Dq=0$.

<a id="env:error-uniform"></a>
<a id="env:radial-error"></a>
Define nonnegative quantities, equal to zero off the radial annuli, $$T=\frac{\chi s}{\epsilon},\qquad
 N=\frac{\chi(s')_-}{\epsilon}.$$ The following estimates hold uniformly, including at the cutoff and annulus boundaries: $$\begin{aligned}
 |\mathcal E|+\eta^2N
 &\le C\eta^{1/2}(1+T),\label{env:error-uniform}\\
 N(\chi s)^2
 &\le\|s'\|_\infty\epsilon T^2,
 \qquad N(\chi s)^2\le K_qT.
 \label{env:radial-error}
\end{aligned}$$ Indeed, $$\frac{\sqrt\chi\,s}{\eta}
   =\eta^{1/2}\sqrt{sT}\le C\eta^{1/2}\sqrt T,
 \qquad
 \eta^2N\le C\eta^{1/2}\sqrt{\chi T},$$ while the other terms in <a href="#env:error-raw" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="env:error-raw">[env:error-raw]</a> are $\eta,\eta^3T,\eta^2$. Finally, $N(\chi s)^2=T\chi^2s(s')_-$, which proves both estimates in <a href="#env:radial-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="env:radial-error">[env:radial-error]</a>. Equation <a href="#env:gradient-expansion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="env:gradient-expansion">[env:gradient-expansion]</a> also gives $d\widetilde f(e_1)=A(1+O(\eta))$; thus the new gradient never vanishes once the cells are sufficiently small.

<a id="pm-h-30"></a>
###### Strictness when $T$ is large.

<a id="env:gradient-fast-lower"></a>
At a point with $T>0$, take the *exactly* transverse vector <a id="env:angular-test"></a>$$\label{env:angular-test}
 v=t-\frac{d\widetilde f(t)}{d\widetilde f(e_1)}e_1,
 \qquad e=\frac{v}{|v|_h}.$$ Since $n^\flat(t)=0$, this is $t+O(\eta)e_1$ before normalization. Its radial component is exactly zero, and normalization in the variable metric is scalar multiplication. Hence $$Q(e,e)=\frac{T}{r|v|_h^2}\ge\frac{T}{2R}$$ for all sufficiently small cells. By <a href="#env:gradient-expansion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="env:gradient-expansion">[env:gradient-expansion]</a>, the radial component of $a_*/A$ is $\delta\chi s+O(\eta)$. Discarding the nonnegative tangential part of $Q$ and using <a href="#env:radial-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="env:radial-error">[env:radial-error]</a> gives $$\label{env:gradient-fast-lower}
 \begin{split}
 Q(a_*,a_*)&\ge
 -A^2\bigl(2\delta^2N(\chi s)^2+C\eta^2N\bigr)\\
 &\ge-A^2\bigl(2\delta^2K_qT+C\eta^2N\bigr).
 \end{split}$$ Also $|a_*|_h^2\ge A^2/2$. For $T\ge1$, <a href="#env:error-uniform" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="env:error-uniform">[env:error-uniform]</a> therefore bounds the contribution of $\delta A(Q+\mathcal E)$ to the strictness expression from below by $$\delta A^3\left(\frac1{4R}-2K_q\delta^2
                         -C\eta^{1/2}\right)T.$$ Here and below the constant $C$ may change with the current envelope; the positive lower bound on $A$ absorbs the factors $1+A^{-2}$ from the error terms. By <a href="#env:fixed-delta" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="env:fixed-delta">[env:fixed-delta]</a>, sufficiently small $\eta$ makes this at least $\delta A^3T/(16R)$. The contribution of the old Hessian $H$ is bounded below by $-C_H$ uniformly in the cell and its point. Choose a finite threshold $$T_0>\max\left\{1,\frac{32RC_H}{\delta\min_{\overline D}|a|_h^3}\right\}.$$ Then <a href="#env:condition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="env:condition">[env:condition]</a> holds at every point where $T\ge T_0$, once the subdivision is sufficiently fine. Only $T_0$ and this fineness threshold depend on the current Hessian and gradient bounds.

<a id="pm-h-31"></a>
###### Strictness when $T$ is bounded.

<a id="env:old-test"></a>
For $0\le T\le T_0$, the radial coefficient $N$ may still be large. We use the reserved transverse direction and bound the negative contribution to $Q(a_*,a_*)$ separately. Set $$\label{env:old-test}
 v=e_2-\frac{d\widetilde f(e_2)}{d\widetilde f(e_1)}e_1,
 \qquad e=\frac{v}{|v|_h}.$$ This vector is exactly transverse, is $e_2+O(\eta)$ after normalization, and has no fast-plane component. Consequently $Q(e,e)=0$ exactly. Moreover $\chi s=\epsilon T\to0$ uniformly in this regime, so $a_*=Ae_1+o(1)$. Equations <a href="#env:gradient-fast-lower" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="env:gradient-fast-lower">[env:gradient-fast-lower]</a> and <a href="#env:radial-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="env:radial-error">[env:radial-error]</a>, using the stronger bounded-$T$ estimate before replacing it by $K_qT$, give $$Q(a_*,a_*)\ge
 -A^2\bigl(2\delta^2\|s'\|_\infty\epsilon T_0^2
                  +C\eta^2N\bigr)=o(1).$$ Also $\mathcal E=o(1)$ uniformly by <a href="#env:error-uniform" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="env:error-uniform">[env:error-uniform]</a>. The old-Hessian contribution, tested with <a href="#env:old-test" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="env:old-test">[env:old-test]</a>, converges uniformly to its value at the cell center, where $e_2$ was chosen to maximize it. Those center values have a common positive lower bound, by strictness of $f$ on $\overline D$. Thus the full expression is positive for sufficiently small $\eta$. The two regimes cover every point of the perturbation; outside its support the original envelope is unchanged.

<a id="pm-h-32"></a>
###### A fixed gain and a small value change.

<a id="env:norm-gain"></a>
In every cell, <a href="#env:gradient-expansion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="env:gradient-expansion">[env:gradient-expansion]</a> implies $$\label{env:norm-gain}
 \frac{|a_*|_h}{A}
   =\sqrt{1+\delta^2\chi^2s^2}+O(\eta),
 \qquad \frac{|a|_h}{A}=1+O(\eta).$$ Choose $s_0>0$ such that the set $s\ge s_0$ has positive area in one fast-variable period. Since $\epsilon/\eta=\eta^2\to0$, periodicity shows that a fixed fraction $\beta>0$ of each whole cell has both $\chi=1$ and $s\ge s_0$. The constant $\beta$ depends only on the fixed cutoffs and metric comparison: the central cutoff box contains many fast periods, and the linear coordinate Jacobians and box-to-cell volume ratios have uniform bounds. Set $$\gamma=\sqrt{1+\delta^2s_0^2}-1>0.$$ Integrating <a href="#env:norm-gain" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="env:norm-gain">[env:norm-gain]</a> on a cell yields $$\int_C|a_*|_h\,dx
 \ge\int_C|a|_h\,dx+A(\beta\gamma-C\eta)|C|.$$ For sufficiently small $\eta$, the last term is at least $A\beta\gamma|C|/2$, while $\int_C|a|_h\,dx\le2A|C|$. Thus <a href="#env:amplification-gain" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="env:amplification-gain">[env:amplification-gain]</a> follows with $d_*=\beta\gamma/4$, independently of the current strictness margin and derivative bounds.

Finally, disjointness of the supports gives $$\|\widetilde f-f\|_\infty
 \le\delta\bigl(\max_C A_C\bigr)\epsilon\|q\|_\infty,$$ which tends to zero with the cell size. This proves the one-step assertion with any budget $b$. For any desired finite integral, a finite number of gains by $1+d_*$ suffices. Assign these finitely many steps portions of the total sup-norm budget and choose each subdivision after fixing its current envelope. Their supports form a finite union compactly contained in $D$. Strictness and nonvanishing hold after every step. This is a finite construction of a smooth envelope, with no limit of envelopes required. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-33"></a>
###### Separated envelope and background regions

<a id="stmt-env-gaps"></a>
<div id="env:gaps" class="lemma">

<a id="env:gap-inequalities"></a>
<a id="env:large-integral"></a>
**Lemma 27** (Gaps and arbitrary growth). *Fix $h\in\mathcal U$ with $\operatorname{supp}(h-g_0)\Subset P$. There are nested open coordinate boxes $$\overline D\cup\operatorname{supp}(h-g_0)\subset U_0
 \Subset U_1\Subset U_2\Subset U_3\Subset U_4\Subset P.$$ For every $L_*>0$ there is a smooth strict envelope $f$ for $h$ on a neighborhood of $\overline P$, and a number $\sigma>0$, such that $$\begin{gathered}
 f>f_0+\sigma\quad\hbox{on }\overline U_0,
 \qquad
 f<f_0-\sigma\quad\hbox{on }\overline P\setminus U_1,
 \label{env:gap-inequalities}\\
 \int_D\sqrt{2+|\nabla_h f|_h^2}\,dx>L_*.
 \label{env:large-integral}
\end{gathered}$$ The envelope and all these patches are fixed before the frequency $k$ is chosen.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-env-gaps"><summary>Proof of stmt-env-gaps</summary>

*Proof.* The indicated compact set lies inside the coordinate cube $P$, so nested coordinate boxes with these strict inclusions exist. Choose $\zeta\in C_c^\infty(U_1)$ equal to one on a neighborhood of $\overline U_0$. For a sufficiently small fixed $\tau>0$, $$f^{(1)}=f_0+\tau(2\zeta-1)$$ is still strict on a neighborhood of $\overline P$, by the $C^2$-openness of <a href="#env:condition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="env:condition">[env:condition]</a>. Its gaps relative to $f_0$ are exactly $\tau$ on $\overline U_0$ and $-\tau$ outside $U_1$. Apply Lemma <a href="#env:amplify" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="env:amplify">26</a> finitely many times in $D$, with total sup-norm change less than $\tau/4$, to make $\int_D|\nabla_hf|_h\,dx>L_*$. The resulting $f$ is unchanged outside a compact subset of $D$ and remains strict everywhere in question. Taking $\sigma=\tau/2$ gives <a href="#env:gap-inequalities" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="env:gap-inequalities">[env:gap-inequalities]</a>; the inequality $B\ge|\nabla_hf|_h$ gives <a href="#env:large-integral" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="env:large-integral">[env:large-integral]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:four-waves"></a>
##### Finite-order waves and real noncancellation

Fix the metric $h$, the envelope $f$, and the nested sets supplied by Lemma <a href="#env:gaps" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="env:gaps">27</a>. We construct real quasimodes with arbitrarily small weighted residual and a quantitative lower bound on their value and first derivative taken together. This lower bound will allow the metric correction to divide locally by either the function or its squared gradient norm. All constants in this section may depend on the fixed data and on the finite accuracy requested below, but the displayed powers in the derivative and noncancellation bounds will not. Differentiation and distances refer to the fixed coordinates on $P$, unless a metric is specified.

<a id="pm-h-35"></a>
###### The localized packets

<a id="wave:transverse"></a>
At each $y\in\overline U_3$, choose three linearly independent vectors $b_j(y)\in a(y)^\perp$, $1\le j\le3$, such that $$|b_j(y)|_h=B(y),\qquad
 H_y(a,a)+H_y(b_j,b_j)>0.
 \label{wave:transverse}$$ Such triples exist: the strict envelope condition gives a nonempty open subset of the sphere of radius $B(y)$ in the three-dimensional space $a(y)^\perp$, and every such open subset contains three linearly independent vectors. Each triple extends smoothly to a neighborhood, by projection into $a^\perp$ and normalization. A finite cover of $\overline U_3$ therefore allows the choices in <a href="#wave:transverse" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="wave:transverse">[wave:transverse]</a> to have uniform positive strictness margins and uniformly positive Gram determinants. We choose one available triple at each center. No regularity of this choice as a function of the center is required. Write $b_j^\flat=h_y(b_j,\cdot)$ and $$c_{yj}=\frac{H_y(a,a)+H_y(b_j,b_j)}{|a(y)|_h^2+|b_j(y)|_h^2}.$$ These numbers have a positive lower bound and a finite upper bound.

<a id="stmt-wave-packets"></a>
<div id="wave:packets" class="proposition">

<a id="wave:gaussian-bounds"></a>
<a id="wave:packet-derivatives"></a>
<a id="wave:packet-residual"></a>
**Proposition 28** (Finite-order packets). *For any integers $\ell,J\ge0$, and all sufficiently large integers $k$, there are packets indexed by $$Y_k=(k^{-1}\mathbb Z^4)\cap U_3,\qquad y\in Y_k,\quad 1\le j\le3,$$ of the form <a id="wave:packet-form"></a>$$p_{yj,k}(x)=e^{k\Phi_{yj}(x)}A_{yj,k}(x)\xi(x-y).
 \label{wave:packet-form}$$ Here $\xi$ is a smooth cutoff supported in a fixed small ball, equal to one in a smaller ball; all translated supporting balls lie in $P$. The phases are complex polynomials in $x-y$, and the amplitudes are polynomials of fixed finite degree in $x-y$ and $k^{-1}$. Their coefficients are uniformly bounded over the centers and indices. They satisfy <a id="wave:packet-jets"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 \Phi_{yj}(y)&=f(y),&
 d\Phi_{yj}(y)&=df(y)+i b_j^\flat,\notag\\
 \Re\mathop{\mathrm{Hess}}_h\Phi_{yj}(y)&=H_y-c_{yj}h_y,&
 A_{yj,k}(y)&=1.
 \label{wave:packet-jets}
\end{aligned}$$ On each supporting ball, for positive constants $c,C$, $$-C|x-y|^2\le\Re\Phi_{yj}(x)-f(x)
       \le-c|x-y|^2,
 \qquad
 A_{yj,k}(x)=1+O(|x-y|+k^{-1}).
 \label{wave:gaussian-bounds}$$ The amplitude and each of its fixed-order derivatives are uniformly bounded. For every fixed multi-index $\alpha$, $$|\partial^\alpha p_{yj,k}(x)|
 \le C_\alpha k^{|\alpha|}
              e^{kf(x)-ck|x-y|^2},
 \label{wave:packet-derivatives}$$ after decreasing $c$ if necessary, and $$\sup_{x\in P}e^{-kf(x)}
 \sum_{|\alpha|\le\ell}
 \big|\partial^\alpha(\Delta_h+\Lambda_k)p_{yj,k}(x)\big|
 \le C_{\ell,J}k^{-J}.
 \label{wave:packet-residual}$$ The radii, coefficients, constants, and frequency threshold are fixed before $k$ varies.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-wave-packets"><summary>Proof of stmt-wave-packets</summary>

<a id="wave:eikonal"></a>
*Proof.* We give a finite Taylor construction using the complex-phase WKB method; compare <a id="pm-cite-27"></a>[[DenckerSjostrandZworski2004](#ref-DenckerSjostrandZworski2004), Section 3]. Fix a center and an index and temporarily suppress them. The eikonal equation is $$\langle d\Phi,d\Phi\rangle_h=-2,
 \label{wave:eikonal}$$ with the complex bilinear extension of the metric pairing. Its constant term holds because $a\perp b$ and $|b|_h^2=2+|a|_h^2$. The degree-one condition is $$(\mathop{\mathrm{Hess}}_h\Phi)_y(a+i b,\cdot)=0.$$ To prescribe the real Hessian, put $U=H_y-c_{yj}h_y$. A real symmetric form $V$ must then satisfy <a id="wave:hessian-compatibility"></a>$$V(b,\cdot)=U(a,\cdot),\qquad
 V(a,\cdot)=-U(b,\cdot).
 \label{wave:hessian-compatibility}$$ The two rows of a symmetric form have precisely one compatibility condition, namely $U(a,a)+U(b,b)=0$. Our choice of $c_{yj}$ gives this identity. Define the remaining block of $V$ on $\{a,b\}^\perp$ to be zero. Since $a,b$ are orthogonal with lengths bounded above and away from zero, this constructs $V$ with uniformly bounded coefficients. Thus $U+iV$ is the required covariant Hessian; the connection converts it to the ordinary quadratic Taylor coefficients of $\Phi$.

<a id="wave:jet-inverse"></a>
For completeness, the higher homogeneous equations are also explicitly soluble. In coordinates write $v=a+i b$ and $D_v=v\cdot\partial$. For a complex homogeneous polynomial $F_q$ of degree $q$, choose a complex linear function $\zeta$ with $D_v\zeta=1$. For example, $$\zeta(z)=\frac{\sum_i\overline{v_i}z_i}{\sum_i|v_i|^2}.$$ Then $$\mathcal R_qF_q
 =\sum_{r=0}^q\frac{(-1)^r\zeta^{r+1}}{(r+1)!}D_v^rF_q,
 \qquad D_v\mathcal R_qF_q=F_q.
 \label{wave:jet-inverse}$$ The identity follows by telescoping after differentiation. This is a right inverse from degree $q$ to degree $q+1$, with bounded norm for each fixed degree, uniformly in our data. For $q\ge2$, the degree-$q$ eikonal equation contains the new degree-$(q+1)$ phase coefficient only as $2D_v$ applied to it. All other coefficients in that equation have already been determined. Hence we can match <a href="#wave:eikonal" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="wave:eikonal">[wave:eikonal]</a> through any prescribed finite Taylor degree.

<a id="wave:transport-identity"></a>
<a id="wave:finite-transports"></a>
The transport operator for the eigenvalue $\Lambda_k=2k^2+k$ is $$\mathcal T=2\mathop{\mathrm{grad}}_h\Phi\cdot\partial+\Delta_h\Phi+1.$$ Indeed, without the cutoff, $$e^{-k\Phi}(\Delta_h+\Lambda_k)(e^{k\Phi}A_k)
 =k^2E A_k+k\mathcal T A_k+\Delta_h A_k,
 \qquad E=\langle d\Phi,d\Phi\rangle_h+2.
 \label{wave:transport-identity}$$ Here is one finite choice of all the truncation orders. Set $$M=J+\ell+1,\qquad d=2J+3\ell+6,\qquad
 n_m=d+2(M-m)\ (0\le m\le M),\qquad N=n_0+1.$$ Construct $\Phi$ of degree $N$ so that $E$ vanishes to order $N$ at the center. With this phase fixed, successively construct polynomials $A^{(m)}$ of degree $n_m$, with $A^{(0)}(y)=1$ and $A^{(m)}(y)=0$ for $m\ge1$, satisfying $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathcal T A^{(0)}&=O(|x-y|^{n_0}),\notag\\
 \mathcal T A^{(m)}+\Delta_h A^{(m-1)}
     &=O(|x-y|^{n_m})\qquad(1\le m\le M).
 \label{wave:finite-transports}
\end{aligned}$$ To see that this is possible, solve the homogeneous equations of degrees $q=0,\ldots,n_m-1$ in order. The new coefficient of $A^{(m)}$ has degree $q+1$ and enters as $2D_v$ applied to it, so <a href="#wave:jet-inverse" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="wave:jet-inverse">[wave:jet-inverse]</a> applies. The source $\Delta_h A^{(m-1)}$ uses coefficients of the preceding amplitude of degree at most $q+2\le n_m+1<n_{m-1}$. Those coefficients have already been retained. The tapering degrees thus ensure that no later truncation changes an earlier equation. Only finitely many jets of the smooth coefficients of $h$ are used. This argument is algebraic and makes no convergence assertion about an infinite formal series.

Set $A_k=\sum_{m=0}^M k^{-m}A^{(m)}$. All its fixed-order derivatives are bounded uniformly for $k\ge1$, and it has the stated value and first-order estimate at the center. Every Taylor remainder in <a href="#wave:finite-transports" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="wave:finite-transports">[wave:finite-transports]</a> vanishes to order at least $d$, and the eikonal remainder vanishes to still higher order. Uniform bounds for their differentiated remainders follow from ordinary smooth Taylor estimates and the finite bounded constructions above.

<a id="wave:phase-expansion"></a>
The function $\Re\Phi-f$ has zero value and differential at the center. Its ordinary Hessian there is $-c_{yj}h_y$: the connection terms cancel because its differential is zero. Uniform Taylor bounds therefore give <a href="#wave:gaussian-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="wave:gaussian-bounds">[wave:gaussian-bounds]</a> after choosing a sufficiently small common radius. They also give the expansion, used below, $$d(\Re\Phi_{yj}-f)(x)
 =-c_{yj}h_y(x-y,\cdot)+O(|x-y|^2).
 \label{wave:phase-expansion}$$ Choose $\xi$ within this radius. Differentiating its product with the exponential and amplitude proves <a href="#wave:packet-derivatives" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="wave:packet-derivatives">[wave:packet-derivatives]</a>.

Finally, for $q\ge0$ the elementary Gaussian bound $$\sup_{r\ge0}r^q e^{-ckr^2}\le C_q k^{-q/2}$$ converts Taylor vanishing into powers of $k^{-1}$. Through $\ell$ derivatives, the terms in <a href="#wave:transport-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="wave:transport-identity">[wave:transport-identity]</a> that contain matched errors are bounded in the $e^{kf}$ scale by $$C k^{2+\ell-(d-\ell)/2}\le Ck^{-J-1}.$$ The last uncancelled term is $k^{-M}\Delta_h A^{(M)}$; its corresponding bound is $Ck^{\ell-M}=Ck^{-J-1}$. Derivatives of $\xi$ are supported at a fixed positive distance from the center and contribute an exponentially small remainder. This proves <a href="#wave:packet-residual" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="wave:packet-residual">[wave:packet-residual]</a>. Increasing the finite Taylor orders changes constants and radii, but introduces no new power of $k$ in <a href="#wave:packet-derivatives" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="wave:packet-derivatives">[wave:packet-derivatives]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-36"></a>
###### A real Gaussian superposition

<a id="wave:random"></a>
Fix a smooth function $\omega$, compactly supported in $U_2$ and equal to one near $\overline U_1$. For independent standard real Gaussian variables $G_{yj},G'_{yj}$ define $$u=u_k^0+\omega\Re\sum_{y\in Y_k}\sum_{j=1}^3
             (G_{yj}+iG'_{yj})p_{yj,k},
 \qquad W=e^{kf}+e^{kf_0}\quad\hbox{on }P.
 \label{wave:random}$$ The added term extends smoothly by zero to $M$. There are $O(k^4)$ real Gaussian coefficients, and their coefficient event $$\mathcal E_k=\{\,|G_{yj}|\le k,\ |G'_{yj}|\le k
                         \text{ for all }y,j\,\}$$ satisfies $\mathbb P(\mathcal E_k^c)\le Ck^4e^{-k^2/2}$.

<a id="stmt-wave-residual"></a>
<div id="wave:residual" class="lemma">

<a id="wave:u-derivatives"></a>
<a id="wave:weighted-residual"></a>
**Lemma 29** (Weighted residual bounds). *For any predetermined integers $\ell,J\ge0$, choose the packets of Proposition <a href="#wave:packets" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="wave:packets">28</a> with derivative order $\ell$ and residual power $J+6$. On $\mathcal E_k$, the function in <a href="#wave:random" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="wave:random">[wave:random]</a> equals $u_k^0$ outside a compact subset of $U_2$, its residual $R=(\Delta_h+\Lambda_k)u$ is compactly supported in $U_2$, and $$\begin{aligned}
 \sup_P W^{-1}|\partial^\alpha u|
      &\le C_\alpha k^{|\alpha|+5}
          &&\text{for every fixed }\alpha,\label{wave:u-derivatives}\\
 \sup_P W^{-1}\sum_{|\alpha|\le\ell}|\partial^\alpha R|
      &\le C_{\ell,J}k^{-J}.
          \label{wave:weighted-residual}
\end{aligned}$$ In particular, the exponents in <a href="#wave:u-derivatives" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="wave:u-derivatives">[wave:u-derivatives]</a> are independent of $\ell,J$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-wave-residual"><summary>Proof of stmt-wave-residual</summary>

*Proof.* Summing <a href="#wave:packet-derivatives" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="wave:packet-derivatives">[wave:packet-derivatives]</a> over $O(k^4)$ packets and using the coefficient bound $k$ proves <a href="#wave:u-derivatives" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="wave:u-derivatives">[wave:u-derivatives]</a>, including the fixed cutoff and the elementary bounds for $u_k^0$. The summed packet residual in <a href="#wave:weighted-residual" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="wave:weighted-residual">[wave:weighted-residual]</a> costs at most five powers of $k$.

There are two other residual terms. The background residual is $$(\Delta_h+\Lambda_k)u_k^0=(\Delta_h-\Delta_{g_0})u_k^0,$$ supported in $\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}(h-g_0)\subset U_0$. Each fixed-order derivative is bounded by a fixed power of $k$ times $e^{kf_0}$. The first gap of Lemma <a href="#env:gaps" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="env:gaps">27</a>, $f>f_0+\sigma$ on $\overline U_0$, makes this exponentially small relative to $W$. The commutator terms $[\Delta_h,\omega]p_{yj,k}$ and their derivatives are supported outside $U_1$. There the opposite gap $f<f_0-\sigma$ makes their polynomial bounds times $e^{kf}$ exponentially small relative to $W$. These are all the residual terms. Their supports, and the exact background equation off $U_0$, prove the support assertion. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-37"></a>
###### Five real directions prevent cancellation

The correction in the next sections needs a real function whose value and gradient do not vanish together, with a quantitative lower bound in the scale $W$. Three transverse phase directions and one displaced packet supply the five independent real directions needed in dimension four.

<a id="stmt-wave-noncancellation"></a>
<div id="wave:noncancellation" class="lemma">

<a id="wave:good-event"></a>
**Lemma 30** (Real noncancellation). *For every fixed choice of the preceding finite accuracy orders, the event $$\mathcal G_k=\mathcal E_k\cap
 \left\{\, |(u(x),k^{-1}du(x))|
                 \ge k^{-200}W(x)
                     \text{ for every }x\in\overline U_2\,\right\}
 \label{wave:good-event}$$ has probability tending to one as $k\to\infty$. The norm in this formula is the Euclidean norm on the value and four coordinate derivatives. The power $200$ is independent of the requested accuracy.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-wave-noncancellation"><summary>Proof of stmt-wave-noncancellation</summary>

*Proof.* Put $\mathcal V_k=W^{-1}(u,k^{-1}du)$. First consider a point where <a id="wave:low-regime"></a>$$e^{kf(x)}<k^{-8}W(x).
 \label{wave:low-regime}$$ On $\mathcal E_k$ the random contribution to the unscaled value and normalized gradient is at most $Ck^5e^{kf(x)}\le Ck^{-3}W(x)$. The background gradient bound in Lemma <a href="#env:background-mode" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="env:background-mode">25</a> gives $$|(u_k^0,k^{-1}du_k^0)|\ge c e^{kf_0(x)}
                       \ge c(1-k^{-8})W(x).$$ Thus $|\mathcal V_k(x)|$ is bounded below by a fixed positive number throughout this regime, for all large $k$.

<a id="wave:high-regime"></a>
It remains to estimate small-ball probabilities at a fixed point $x\in\overline U_2$ with $$e^{kf(x)}\ge k^{-8}W(x).
 \label{wave:high-regime}$$ The gap $f<f_0-\sigma$ outside $U_1$ implies that, for large $k$, such a point lies where $\omega=1$ on a neighborhood. Thus no derivative of $\omega$ enters this calculation. Choose a grid center $y$ within $Ck^{-1}$ of $x$, and another center $y'$ within $Ck^{-1}$ of $$x+k^{-1/2}\nu,\qquad \nu=\frac{a(x)}{|a(x)|_h},$$ viewing the tangent vector in the fixed coordinates. The compact inclusion $\overline U_2\subset U_3$ ensures that both centers belong to $Y_k$. They are distinct for all large $k$. The cutoff $\xi$ is one at $x$ for each selected packet.

For the three packets at $y$ and the packet with index $1$ at $y'$, the positive numbers $$\beta_{zj}=e^{-kf(x)}|p_{zj,k}(x)|\qquad(z=y\text{ or }y')$$ are bounded above and away from zero. This follows from both sides of <a href="#wave:gaussian-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="wave:gaussian-bounds">[wave:gaussian-bounds]</a>, since $|x-z|=O(k^{-1/2})$, and from $A_{zj,k}(x)=1+O(k^{-1/2})$.

At this fixed evaluation point, rotate the two Gaussian coordinates of each selected packet by the argument of $p_{zj,k}(x)$. This is a fixed orthogonal change of its two real coordinates, preserving their independent standard Gaussian distribution. After factoring out $\beta_{zj}$, the two columns of the evaluation map in the $e^{kf(x)}$ scale are, up to changing the sign of the second coordinate, <a id="wave:real-columns"></a>$$\begin{pmatrix}1\\
 d\Re\Phi_{zj}(x)+k^{-1}\Re(A_{zj,k}^{-1}dA_{zj,k})(x)
 \end{pmatrix},\qquad
 \begin{pmatrix}0\\
 d\Im\Phi_{zj}(x)+k^{-1}\Im(A_{zj,k}^{-1}dA_{zj,k})(x)
 \end{pmatrix}.
 \label{wave:real-columns}$$ The rotation is held fixed in computing these derivatives. The logarithmic amplitude derivatives appearing here are uniformly bounded at the selected points.

Select the first column for the packet $(y,1)$, the second columns for $(y,1),(y,2),(y,3)$, and the first column for $(y',1)$. These are five independent real Gaussian coordinates: the first two come from the same orthogonally rotated Gaussian pair, and the other three come from different pairs. The near-center columns have the forms $$(1,df(x)+O(k^{-1})),\qquad
 (0,b_j^\flat(y)+O(k^{-1}))\quad(1\le j\le3).$$ For the displaced first column, <a href="#wave:phase-expansion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="wave:phase-expansion">[wave:phase-expansion]</a> gives $$\begin{aligned}
 d\Re\Phi_{y'1}(x)-df(x)
 &=-c_{y'1}h_{y'}(x-y',\cdot)+O(k^{-1})\\
 &=k^{-1/2}c_{y'1}h_x(\nu,\cdot)+O(k^{-1}).
\end{aligned}$$ Subtracting the near first column from this column leaves zero in the value component. Multiplying the resulting column by $k^{1/2}$ leaves the derivative covector $c_{y'1}h_x(\nu,\cdot)+O(k^{-1/2})$. The other three derivative covectors are uniformly independent in $a(y)^\perp$. Since $|x-y|=O(k^{-1})$, they remain uniformly independent together with $h_x(\nu,\cdot)$. This comparison uses smoothness of $a,h$, and does not compare the triples chosen at different centers. The determinant of these five amplitude-factored columns is therefore at least $ck^{-1/2}$ in absolute value. Restoring the four bounded positive packet amplitudes, with the amplitude of $(y,1)$ used twice, preserves this lower bound.

<a id="wave:small-ball"></a>
Changing the normalization from $e^{kf(x)}$ to $W(x)$ multiplies each of the five columns by $e^{kf(x)}/W(x)\ge k^{-8}$. Their determinant in the $W$ scale is consequently at least $ck^{-40-1/2}$. Condition on all the unused rotated Gaussian coordinates. The selected five are still independent standard real Gaussians; the conditioned coordinates and the deterministic background give an arbitrary translation. Change of variables in their bounded Gaussian density now yields, unconditionally as well, $$\mathbb P\{\,|\mathcal V_k(x)|<r\,\}
       \le C k^{41}r^5\qquad(r>0)
 \label{wave:small-ball}$$ at every point in <a href="#wave:high-regime" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="wave:high-regime">[wave:high-regime]</a>. This estimate has not conditioned on the coefficient event $\mathcal E_k$.

Take a net of mesh at most $k^{-208}$ in the compact coordinate set $\overline U_2$. It has $O(k^{832})$ points. At its high-regime points apply <a href="#wave:small-ball" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="wave:small-ball">[wave:small-ball]</a> with $r=2k^{-200}$. The probability of any such small value is at most $$C k^{832+41-1000}=Ck^{-127}.$$ At its low-regime points the preceding deterministic bound applies on $\mathcal E_k$. Thus, outside an event of probability tending to zero, all net values have norm at least $2k^{-200}$ and $\mathcal E_k$ holds.

To pass from the net to the whole set, note that $$W^{-1}|\partial^\alpha W|\le C_\alpha k^{|\alpha|},\qquad
 |d\log W|\le Ck.$$ Together with <a href="#wave:u-derivatives" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="wave:u-derivatives">[wave:u-derivatives]</a> through order two, these give $\|d\mathcal V_k\|\le Ck^6$ on $\mathcal E_k$. The net interpolation error is at most $Ck^{6-208}=Ck^{-202}$, which is smaller than $k^{-200}$ for all large $k$. This proves <a href="#wave:good-event" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="wave:good-event">[wave:good-event]</a>, even when a point and its chosen net point belong to different regimes. Intersecting the coefficient and net events used only a union bound, so no assertion about a truncated Gaussian density is needed. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:signs"></a>
##### Stable sign data and nodal measure

Fix the data of Lemma <a href="#env:gaps" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="env:gaps">27</a>, and recall that $B=\sqrt{2+|\nabla_h f|_h^2}$. We select one of the real quasimodes from Section 8 with many strict opposite-sign tests along short coordinate segments. The score bounds nodal measure from below, and every fixed strict lower bound for it survives sufficiently small uniform perturbations of the function. All limits in this section hold with the data and requested packet accuracy fixed. The positive constants in the score and measure bounds, however, depend only on the fixed chart and metric comparability throughout $\mathcal U$.

<a id="stmt-score-definition"></a>
<div id="score:definition" class="definition">

<a id="score:formula"></a>
**Definition 31** (Sign score). Subdivide $D$, up to its grid boundaries, into finitely many disjoint open coordinate cubes $Q$, fine enough that numbers $B_Q>0$ satisfy $$B_Q\le B(x)\le 2B_Q\qquad (x\in Q).$$ Choose $c_0>0$ as in Lemma <a href="#score:expectation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="score:expectation">33</a> below and put $$\ell_Q=\frac{c_0}{kB_Q},\qquad
 Q^- =\{x\in Q:\operatorname{dist}_{\infty}(x,\partial Q)>\ell_Q\},
 \qquad T_k=\sum_Q\ell_Q^{-1}|Q|.$$ For a real continuous function $\psi$ on $M$, define $$\label{score:formula}
 \mathcal S(\psi)=\sum_Q\ell_Q^{-1}
 \int_{Q^-}\sum_{i=1}^4
 \mathbf 1_{\{\psi(x)\psi(x+\ell_Q d_i)<0\}}\,dx,$$ where $d_i$ are the four coordinate unit vectors and $|Q|$ denotes coordinate volume. The subdivision and $k$ are part of the score’s definition and remain fixed when $\psi$ varies.

</div>

<a id="score:lsc"></a>
In particular, $0\le\mathcal S(\psi)\le4T_k$. If $\psi_n\to\psi$ uniformly, every strictly negative endpoint product for $\psi$ is eventually negative for $\psi_n$. Fatou’s Lemma gives $$\label{score:lsc}
 \mathcal S(\psi)\le\liminf_{n\to\infty}\mathcal S(\psi_n).$$ Multiplication by a positive continuous function, or by a nonzero real constant, preserves every endpoint-sign comparison and hence the score.

<a id="stmt-score-measure"></a>
<div id="score:measure" class="lemma">

<a id="score:hausdorff"></a>
**Lemma 32** (The score bounds nodal measure). *There is $c_{\mathrm H}>0$, independent of the subdivision, $k$, and the envelope, such that, for every $g\in\mathcal U$ and every real $\psi\in C(M)$, $$\label{score:hausdorff}
 \mathcal H_g^3(\{\psi=0\})\ge c_{\mathrm H}\mathcal S(\psi).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-score-measure"><summary>Proof of stmt-score-measure</summary>

<a id="score:sweep"></a>
*Proof.* Write $Z=\{\psi=0\}$ and, for fixed $Q,i$, let $E_{Qi}$ be the set indicated in the integrand of <a href="#score:formula" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="score:formula">[score:formula]</a>. This is a Borel set. The full segment from $x$ to $x+\ell_Qd_i$ lies in $Q$ when $x\in Q^-$. The Intermediate Value Theorem therefore gives, in coordinate space, $$E_{Qi}\subset (Z\cap Q)-[0,\ell_Q]d_i.$$ Cover $Z\cap Q$ by countably many Euclidean balls of radii $r_j<\delta$. The backward sweep of each ball is a cylinder with rounded ends whose four-dimensional volume is at most $C(\ell_Qr_j^3+r_j^4)$. Countable subadditivity yields $$|E_{Qi}|\le C(\ell_Q+\delta)\sum_jr_j^3.$$ If the coordinate Hausdorff three-measure of $Z\cap Q$ is finite, take the infimum over such covers and then let $\delta\downarrow0$. The definition of Hausdorff measure, with the harmless dimensional factor converting diameters to ball radii, gives $$\label{score:sweep}
 \ell_Q^{-1}|E_{Qi}|
 \le C\mathcal H^3_{\mathrm{Eucl}}(Z\cap Q).$$ If this Hausdorff measure is infinite, the same inequality is immediate. This proof uses only measurable covering cylinders and does not require any regularity of $Z$.

To compare the two Hausdorff measures, multiply each coordinate function by a smooth cutoff equal to one near $\overline D$ and compactly supported in $P$, and extend by zero to $M$. The resulting smooth map $F:M\to\mathbb R^4$ agrees with the coordinate map near $\overline D$. Uniform metric comparability bounds $|dF|_g$ by a fixed constant. Integrating along curves shows that $F$ is globally Lipschitz for the Riemannian distance of every $g\in\mathcal U$. Thus $$\mathcal H^3_{\mathrm{Eucl}}(Z\cap Q)
 \le C\mathcal H_g^3(Z\cap Q).$$ Sum <a href="#score:sweep" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="score:sweep">[score:sweep]</a> over the four directions and the disjoint open cubes. The sets $Z\cap Q$ are disjoint Borel sets, so their Hausdorff measures sum to at most $\mathcal H_g^3(Z)$. This proves <a href="#score:hausdorff" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="score:hausdorff">[score:hausdorff]</a>, including the case of infinite measure. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-score-expectation"></a>
<div id="score:expectation" class="lemma">

<a id="score:expected"></a>
**Lemma 33** (Expected score). *One can choose $c_0>0$ and $c_{\mathrm E}>0$, using only uniform metric comparability, such that the Gaussian superposition <a href="#wave:random" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="wave:random">[wave:random]</a> satisfies, for all sufficiently large integers $k$, $$\label{score:expected}
 \mathbb E\mathcal S(u)\ge c_{\mathrm E}T_k.$$ The threshold for $k$ may depend on all fixed data, including the envelope and the packet accuracy, whereas $c_0,c_{\mathrm E}$ do not.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-score-expectation"><summary>Proof of stmt-score-expectation</summary>

*Proof.* On $D$ the macroscopic cutoff is one. Normalize the random part by $e^{kf(x)}$ and write $$v_{yj}(x)=e^{-kf(x)}p_{yj,k}(x),\qquad
 X(x)=\operatorname{Re}\sum_{y,j}(G_{yj}+iG'_{yj})v_{yj}(x).$$ The two Gaussian coefficients of each packet are independent, so <a id="score:covariance"></a>$$\label{score:covariance}
 \operatorname{Var}X(x)=\sum_{y,j}|v_{yj}(x)|^2,\qquad
 \operatorname{Cov}(X(x),X(x'))
 =\sum_{y,j}\operatorname{Re}\bigl(v_{yj}(x)\overline{v_{yj}(x')}\bigr).$$ The nearest grid center is at distance $O(k^{-1})$ from $x$ and lies in $U_3$. The two-sided quadratic phase bounds and the amplitude estimate in Proposition <a href="#wave:packets" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="wave:packets">28</a> show that its normalized packet has absolute value bounded below by a positive constant. Hence $\operatorname{Var}X(x)\ge c>0$, uniformly on $D$ for the fixed data.

Fix $x\in Q^-$ and put $x_i=x+\ell_Qd_i$. Call a packet near if $|y-x|\le k^{-2/5}$. For all other packets, both $x$ and $x_i$ are at distance at least $\tfrac12 k^{-2/5}$ from the center for large $k$. The Gaussian decay and the $O(k^4)$ packet count bound their total contributions to the variances and covariances by $Ck^4e^{-ck^{1/5}}$. These are negligible even relative to the positive variance lower bound.

<a id="score:endpoints"></a>
For near packets the cutoff is one at both endpoints. Put $\beta_{yj}=b_j^\flat(y)$. Along the segment, $$d(\Phi_{yj}-f)=i\beta_{yj}+O(k^{-2/5}).$$ The packet amplitudes are $1+O(|x-y|+k^{-1})$, so their endpoint ratios tend uniformly to one. Since $k\ell_Q=c_0/B_Q$ is bounded, integration of the preceding differential identity gives $$\label{score:endpoints}
 v_{yj}(x_i)=v_{yj}(x)
 \left(\exp\!\left(\frac{ic_0\beta_{yj}(d_i)}{B_Q}\right)+o(1)\right).$$ Every error here is uniform over the indicated points and packets for the fixed data; smooth dependence of $b_j(y)$ on the center is not needed.

<a id="score:correlation"></a>
Let $S_0=\sum_{\mathrm{near}}|v_{yj}(x)|^2$. The nearest packet gives $S_0\ge c$, while <a href="#score:endpoints" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="score:endpoints">[score:endpoints]</a> and the tail estimate show that the two endpoint variances are $S_0(1+o(1))$. Thus their centered Gaussian correlation is $$\label{score:correlation}
 \mathcal R_i=
 \sum_{\mathrm{near}}\frac{|v_{yj}(x)|^2}{S_0}
 \cos\!\left(\frac{c_0\beta_{yj}(d_i)}{B_Q}\right)+o(1).$$

Uniform metric comparability, $|b_j(y)|_h=B(y)$, and continuity of the fixed $B$ give constants $a_0,a_1>0$, independent of the envelope, with $$a_0B_Q\le|\beta_{yj}|_{\mathrm{Eucl}}\le a_1B_Q
 \qquad\text{for every near packet and all sufficiently large }k.$$ Indeed $B(x)\in[B_Q,2B_Q]$, and $B(y)/B(x)\to1$ uniformly when $|y-x|\le k^{-2/5}$. Choose $c_0a_1\le1$. The elementary inequality $1-\cos s\ge s^2/4$ for $|s|\le1$ implies $$\sum_{i=1}^4\left(1-\cos\frac{c_0\beta_{yj}(d_i)}{B_Q}\right)
 \ge\frac{c_0^2|\beta_{yj}|_{\mathrm{Eucl}}^2}{4B_Q^2}
 \ge\kappa>0,$$ where $\kappa$ is independent of the envelope. The weights in <a href="#score:correlation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="score:correlation">[score:correlation]</a> are the same for all four coordinate directions. Weighted averaging therefore gives $\sum_i(1-\mathcal R_i)\ge\kappa/2$, so for at least one $i$, $\mathcal R_i\le1-\kappa/8$. This bound uses the length of each carrier covector, not the Gram determinant of the triple chosen at its center; hence $\kappa$ is uniform over the amplified envelopes.

For centered Gaussian variables with positive variances and correlation $r\in[-1,1]$, the probability of opposite signs is $\arccos(r)/\pi$. To see this, express the variables as projections of a standard planar Gaussian onto unit vectors with angle $\arccos r$ and use rotation invariance; the degenerate endpoints follow directly. Therefore $$\sum_{i=1}^4\mathbb P\{X(x)X(x_i)<0\}\ge q_0>0$$ with $q_0$ independent of the envelope.

The normalized deterministic mean is $m(x)=e^{k(f_0(x)-f(x))}\cos(k\phi(x))$, with $|m(x)|\le e^{-k\sigma}$ on $D$ by Lemma <a href="#env:gaps" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="env:gaps">27</a>. For a centered one-dimensional Gaussian of standard deviation $s>0$, adding a mean $m$ changes its sign with probability at most $\mathbb P\{|X|\le|m|\}\le C|m|/s$. The variance lower bounds at both endpoints consequently change each opposite-sign probability by $o(1)$, uniformly in $x$. Thus $$\sum_{i=1}^4\mathbb P\{u(x)u(x_i)<0\}\ge q_0/2$$ for large $k$. Tonelli’s Theorem and $|Q^-|/|Q|\to1$ for the fixed finite subdivision now prove <a href="#score:expected" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="score:expected">[score:expected]</a> with, for example, $c_{\mathrm E}=q_0/4$. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-score-good"></a>
<div id="score:good" class="proposition">

<a id="score:residual"></a>
<a id="score:large"></a>
**Proposition 34** (A simultaneous good realization). *For every predetermined pair of finite orders $\ell,J\ge0$, all sufficiently large integers $k$ admit a choice of the real Gaussian coefficients for which the smooth real function $u$ satisfies <a id="score:support"></a><a id="score:bounds"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 &u=u_k^0\quad\text{outside a compact subset of }U_2,
 \qquad \mathop{\mathrm{supp}}R\Subset U_2,
 \qquad R=(\Delta_h+\Lambda_k)u,\label{score:support}\\
 &\sup_P W^{-1}\sum_{|\alpha|\le\ell}|\partial^\alpha R|
 \le C_{\ell,J}k^{-J},\label{score:residual}\\
 &W^{-1}|\partial^\alpha u|\le C_\alpha k^{|\alpha|+5}
 \quad\text{on }P\text{ for every fixed multi-index }\alpha,\notag\\
 &|(u,k^{-1}du)|\ge k^{-200}W
 \quad\text{on }\overline U_2,\label{score:bounds}\\
 &\mathcal S(u)\ge c_{\mathrm s}k\int_D B(x)\,dx.
 \label{score:large}
\end{aligned}$$ Here $c_{\mathrm s}>0$ is independent of the envelope and the accuracy orders. The constants $C_{\ell,J},C_\alpha$ and the frequency threshold may depend on these fixed data, but the polynomial exponents in these bounds do not increase with the residual accuracy.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-score-good"><summary>Proof of stmt-score-good</summary>

*Proof.* Choose the packet accuracy in Lemma <a href="#wave:residual" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="wave:residual">29</a> to give <a href="#score:residual" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="score:residual">[score:residual]</a>. Let $G_k$ be the event in Lemma <a href="#wave:noncancellation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="wave:noncancellation">30</a>: all coefficients have absolute value at most $k$, and the stated noncancellation bound holds. Its probability tends to one, and its coefficient bound gives the support, residual, and derivative conclusions above. By Lemma <a href="#score:expectation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="score:expectation">33</a> and the deterministic upper bound $\mathcal S\le4T_k$, $$\mathbb E\bigl[\mathcal S(u)\mathbf1_{G_k}\bigr]
 \ge\bigl(c_{\mathrm E}-4\mathbb P(G_k^c)\bigr)T_k
 \ge\frac{c_{\mathrm E}}2T_k$$ for all large $k$. Hence there is a realization in $G_k$ with $\mathcal S(u)\ge c_{\mathrm E}T_k/4$. This conclusion uses no independence between the score and $G_k$. Finally, $$T_k=\frac{k}{c_0}\sum_QB_Q|Q|
 \ge\frac{k}{2c_0}\int_D B(x)\,dx,$$ so <a href="#score:large" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="score:large">[score:large]</a> holds with $c_{\mathrm s}=c_{\mathrm E}/(8c_0)$. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:correction"></a>
##### Realization by an ordinary metric

We now turn a sufficiently accurate real quasimode into an exact eigenfunction of an ordinary four-dimensional metric. The construction preserves signs and retains a region on which both the metric and the eigenfunction are the explicit background ones. In this section all $C^r$ norms are taken in fixed finite coordinate charts. Constants may depend on the fixed background and patches, but not on $k$.

<a id="stmt-corr-realization"></a>
<div id="corr:realization" class="lemma">

<a id="corr:support"></a>
<a id="corr:derivatives"></a>
<a id="corr:noncancellation"></a>
<a id="corr:residual"></a>
**Lemma 35** (Ordinary-metric realization). *Fix the background $M,g_0,P,u_k^0,\Lambda_k$ of Lemma <a href="#env:background-mode" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="env:background-mode">25</a>, patches $U_2\Subset U_3\Subset U_4\Subset P$, a smooth metric $h$ such that $h-g_0$ has compact support in $U_2$, and a fixed smooth function $f$ on a neighborhood of $\overline P$. Put $$W=e^{kf}+e^{kf_0}.$$ For every integer $p\ge0$ and every $L>0$, there are finite integers $\ell,J$ with the following property. Suppose smooth real functions $u=u_k$, for arbitrarily large integers $k$, satisfy $$\begin{aligned}
 &\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}(u-u_k^0)\Subset U_2,
 \qquad \mathop{\mathrm{supp}}R\Subset U_2,
 \qquad R=(\Delta_h+\Lambda_k)u, \label{corr:support}\tag{C1}\\
 &W^{-1}|\partial^\alpha u|
       \le C_\alpha k^{|\alpha|+5}
       \quad\hbox{on }P,\quad |\alpha|\le\ell+1,
       \label{corr:derivatives}\tag{C2}\\
 &\bigl(u^2+k^{-2}|du|_{\mathrm{Euc}}^2\bigr)^{1/2}
       \ge k^{-200}W
       \quad\hbox{on }\overline U_2,\label{corr:noncancellation}\tag{C3}\\
 &W^{-1}|\partial^\alpha R|
       \le C_R k^{-J}
       \quad\hbox{on }P,\quad |\alpha|\le\ell.
       \label{corr:residual}\tag{C4}
\end{aligned}$$ The constants in these estimates are independent of $k$; the supports in <a href="#corr:support" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="corr:support">[corr:support]</a> need not be independent of $k$. For all sufficiently large such $k$, there are a smooth ordinary metric $g_*$ and a smooth positive function $b_*$ on $M$ such that $$\|g_*-h\|_{C^p}=O(k^{-L}),\qquad
 t=b_*u,\qquad (\Delta_{g_*}+\Lambda_k)t=0.$$ Moreover, $g_*-g_0$ and $t-u_k^0$ have compact support in $U_4$. In particular, the zero set and every strict sign comparison of $u$ are preserved.*

</div>

The preceding sections supply all the hypotheses of Lemma <a href="#corr:realization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="corr:realization">35</a>. Its proof below does not use the strict envelope inequality; that inequality was needed to construct the quasimode. The essential geometric input here is the clean collar $U_4\setminus\overline U_2$, where $h=g_0$, $u=u_k^0$, and the background gradient has a quantitative lower bound.

<a id="pm-h-40"></a>
###### Exact correction with an independent density

We first allow the principal tensor and divergence density to vary independently. The scalar $\vartheta$ below is a density increment; the symbol $d$ continues to denote differentiation.

<a id="stmt-corr-weighted"></a>
<div id="corr:weighted" class="lemma">

<a id="corr:weighted-equation"></a>
<a id="corr:weighted-estimate"></a>
**Lemma 36** (Weighted correction). *Under <a href="#corr:support" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="corr:support">[corr:support]</a>–<a href="#corr:residual" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="corr:residual">[corr:residual]</a>, there are a smooth symmetric contravariant tensor $F$ and a smooth scalar $\vartheta$, both compactly supported in $U_2$, such that $$\label{corr:weighted-equation}
 \mathop{\mathrm{div}}_h\bigl((h^{-1}+F)\,du\bigr)
       +\Lambda_k(1+\vartheta)u=0.$$ For each integer $0\le r\le\ell-1$, their estimates through order $r$ use only derivatives of $u$ through order $r+2$ and of $R$ through order $r+1$. A fixed finite exponent $E_r$, independent of $J$, satisfies $$\label{corr:weighted-estimate}
 \|F\|_{C^r}+\|\vartheta\|_{C^r}
       \le C_r k^{E_r-J};
 \qquad E_r=1000(r+2)\quad\hbox{is sufficient}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-corr-weighted"><summary>Proof of stmt-corr-weighted</summary>

<a id="corr:theta"></a>
*Proof.* Choose a fixed smooth function $\gamma_0:\mathbb R\to[0,1]$, equal to one on $[-1/4,1/4]$ and supported in $(-1/2,1/2)$, and set on $U_2$ $$\gamma=\gamma_0(k^{200}u/W).$$ On the support of $\gamma$, <a href="#corr:noncancellation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="corr:noncancellation">[corr:noncancellation]</a> gives $|du|_h\ge c k^{-199}W$. Wherever $1-\gamma\ne0$, $|u|\ge\tfrac14 k^{-200}W$. Define on $U_2$ <a id="corr:F"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 F&=-u\gamma R|du|_h^{-2}h^{-1},\label{corr:F}\\
 \vartheta&=\Lambda_k^{-1}\left(
       -(1-\gamma)\frac R u
       +\mathop{\mathrm{div}}_h\bigl(\gamma R|du|_h^{-2}\mathop{\mathrm{grad}}_hu\bigr)
                       \right).\label{corr:theta}
\end{aligned}$$ Each quotient is used only where its denominator is nonzero. Extend the cutoff quotient by zero on the unused part of its domain. This is smooth: near a zero of $du$, the first cutoff is identically zero, and near a zero of $u$, the second cutoff is identically zero. The support condition on $R$ then permits smooth extension of both corrections by zero to $M$, including the divergence term.

Put $q=\gamma R|du|_h^{-2}$. The product rule gives the exact identities $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathop{\mathrm{div}}_h(Fdu)&=-\gamma R-u\mathop{\mathrm{div}}_h(q\mathop{\mathrm{grad}}_hu),\\
 \Lambda_k\vartheta u&=-(1-\gamma)R
                         +u\mathop{\mathrm{div}}_h(q\mathop{\mathrm{grad}}_hu).
\end{aligned}$$ Adding these identities to the definition of $R$ proves <a href="#corr:weighted-equation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="corr:weighted-equation">[corr:weighted-equation]</a>, also at the zeros of $u$, by smoothness.

<a id="corr:normalized-F"></a>
<a id="corr:normalized-quotients"></a>
For the estimates, use normalized quantities $$a_0=u/W,\qquad \beta=du/W,\qquad r_0=R/W.$$ All exponentially varying factors cancel from the formulas: $$\begin{aligned}
 F&=-a_0\gamma r_0|\beta|_h^{-2}h^{-1},\label{corr:normalized-F}\\
 q\mathop{\mathrm{grad}}_hu&=\gamma r_0|\beta|_h^{-2}\beta^{\sharp_h},
 \qquad (1-\gamma)R/u=(1-\gamma)r_0/a_0.
 \label{corr:normalized-quotients}
\end{aligned}$$ Since $f,f_0$ are fixed, differentiation of their exponentials and the reciprocal of their positive sum gives $$|\partial^jW|\le C_j k^jW,
 \qquad |\partial^j(W^{-1})|\le C_j k^jW^{-1}.$$ Consequently, for every required order $j$, <a id="corr:normalized-bounds"></a>$$\label{corr:normalized-bounds}
 |\partial^j a_0|\le C_jk^{j+5},\qquad
 |\partial^j\beta|\le C_jk^{j+6},\qquad
 |\partial^j r_0|\le C_jk^{j-J}.$$ Here and below a bound on $\partial^j$ means the bound for every multi-index of that length. The chain and quotient rules give, on the respective cutoff domains, $$\begin{aligned}
 |\partial^j\gamma|&\le C_jk^{206j},\quad j\ge1,\\
 |\partial^j(a_0^{-1})|&\le C_jk^{200+206j},\\
 |\partial^j(|\beta|_h^{-2})|&\le C_jk^{398+411j}.
\end{aligned}$$ For example, $|\beta|_h^2\ge c k^{-398}$ on the first domain, and $|\partial^j(|\beta|_h^2)|\le C_jk^{j+12}$. Each term in its differentiated reciprocal has at most $j$ differentiated factors, which proves the last displayed bound. These deliberately generous powers also bound derivatives at cutoff interfaces. Substitution into <a href="#corr:normalized-F" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="corr:normalized-F">[corr:normalized-F]</a>–<a href="#corr:normalized-quotients" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="corr:normalized-quotients">[corr:normalized-quotients]</a>, with one extra derivative for the divergence in <a href="#corr:theta" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="corr:theta">[corr:theta]</a>, proves <a href="#corr:weighted-estimate" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="corr:weighted-estimate">[corr:weighted-estimate]</a>. In particular, the loss depends only on the requested derivative order and the fixed exponent in <a href="#corr:noncancellation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="corr:noncancellation">[corr:noncancellation]</a>, and does not grow when $J$ is increased. ◻

</details>

<a id="corr:potential-equation"></a>
<a id="corr:potential-estimate"></a>
Take the residual accuracy high enough that $F$ and $\vartheta$ are small. Then $h^{-1}+F$ is positive definite and $1+\vartheta>0$. Define an ordinary metric $h_1$, a positive weight $w$, a real function $v$, and a potential $V_0$ by <a id="corr:conjugation-data"></a>$$\label{corr:conjugation-data}
 \begin{aligned}
 h_1^{-1}&=\frac{h^{-1}+F}{1+\vartheta},&
 w&=(1+\vartheta)\frac{d\mathrm{vol}_h}{d\mathrm{vol}_{h_1}},\\
 v&=w^{1/2}u,&
 V_0&=\frac{\Delta_{h_1}(w^{1/2})}{w^{1/2}}.
 \end{aligned}$$ The quotient of volume elements means the quotient of their positive densities. Dividing <a href="#corr:weighted-equation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="corr:weighted-equation">[corr:weighted-equation]</a> by its density yields $$\Delta_{h_1}u+\langle d\log w,du\rangle_{h_1}
                    +\Lambda_ku=0.$$ If $s_0=w^{1/2}$, the product rule cancels the first-order term: $$\Delta_{h_1}(s_0u)
   =s_0\Delta_{h_1}u+2\langle ds_0,du\rangle_{h_1}
                         +u\Delta_{h_1}s_0
   =(V_0-\Lambda_k)s_0u.$$ Thus $$\label{corr:potential-equation}
 \Delta_{h_1}v=(V_0-\Lambda_k)v.$$ The quantities $h_1-h,w-1,V_0$ have compact support in $U_2$, and $v=u_k^0$ outside a compact subset of $U_2$. For any fixed integer $m$, smooth matrix inversion, the determinant formula for volume, and two differentiations in $V_0$ give $$\label{corr:potential-estimate}
 \|h_1-h\|_{C^{m+2}}+\|w-1\|_{C^{m+2}}+\|V_0\|_{C^m}
 \le C_m\bigl(\|F\|_{C^{m+2}}+
                         \|\vartheta\|_{C^{m+2}}\bigr),$$ whenever the right-hand side is sufficiently small.

<a id="pm-h-41"></a>
###### A conformal equation with a coercive linearization

<a id="corr:Z-equation"></a>
For a positive function $Z$, the four-dimensional conformal transformation $g=Z^2h_1$, $t=v/Z$, gives <a id="corr:conformal-identity"></a>$$\label{corr:conformal-identity}
 \Delta_gt
   =Z^{-4}\mathop{\mathrm{div}}_{h_1}\bigl(Z^2\mathop{\mathrm{grad}}_{h_1}(v/Z)\bigr)
   =Z^{-4}\bigl(Z\Delta_{h_1}v-v\Delta_{h_1}Z\bigr).$$ Together with <a href="#corr:potential-equation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="corr:potential-equation">[corr:potential-equation]</a>, this shows that the equation $$\label{corr:Z-equation}
 \Delta_{h_1}Z=V_0Z+\Lambda_k(Z^3-Z)$$ is sufficient for $(\Delta_g+\Lambda_k)t=0$.

<a id="stmt-corr-conformal"></a>
<div id="corr:conformal" class="lemma">

<a id="corr:Z-smallness"></a>
**Lemma 37** (The conformal factor). *Fix the smooth metric $h$ on the closed four-manifold $M$, and an even integer $s>2$. There are $\delta>0$ and $C_s$, depending only on $h,s$ and the fixed Sobolev norms, with the following property. If $h_1,V_0$ are smooth, $$\|h_1-h\|_{C^s}+\|V_0\|_{H^s}\le\delta,
 \qquad \Lambda\ge1,$$ then $$\Delta_{h_1}Z=V_0Z+\Lambda(Z^3-Z)$$ has a smooth positive solution satisfying $$\label{corr:Z-smallness}
 \|Z-1\|_{H^s}\le C_s\Lambda^{-1}\|V_0\|_{H^s}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-corr-conformal"><summary>Proof of stmt-corr-conformal</summary>

<a id="corr:mass-inverse-one"></a>
<a id="corr:mass-inverse-two"></a>
*Proof.* Use the real Sobolev spaces of the fixed metric $h$. The standard elliptic spectral and Sobolev theorems give the eigenbasis for $-\Delta_h$, the equivalence of the usual even-order Sobolev norms with the norms defined by powers of $1-\Delta_h$, and the algebra property of $H^s$ for $s>2$ <a id="pm-cite-28"></a>[[Taylor2011](#ref-Taylor2011), [TaylorPsido](#ref-TaylorPsido)]. If $\mu\ge0$ is a spectral value of $-\Delta_h$, the inverse of $L_\Lambda=\Delta_h-2\Lambda$ has multiplier $-1/(\mu+2\Lambda)$. In the spectral Sobolev norms, therefore, $$\begin{aligned}
 \|L_\Lambda^{-1}\|_{H^s\to H^s}
       &\le (2\Lambda)^{-1},\label{corr:mass-inverse-one}\\
 \|L_\Lambda^{-1}\|_{H^{s-2}\to H^s}
       &\le\sup_{\mu\ge0}\frac{1+\mu}{\mu+2\Lambda}
        \le1.\label{corr:mass-inverse-two}
\end{aligned}$$ Changing to the fixed coordinate Sobolev norms costs constants independent of $\Lambda$.

<a id="corr:fixed-point"></a>
Writing $Z=1+Y$, the required fixed-point equation is $$\label{corr:fixed-point}
 Y=L_\Lambda^{-1}\left[
    (\Delta_h-\Delta_{h_1})Y+V_0(1+Y)
                            +\Lambda(3Y^2+Y^3)\right].$$ The coefficient formula for a Laplacian and the usual integer-order product rule show $$\|\Delta_h-\Delta_{h_1}\|_{H^s\to H^{s-2}}
                         \le C_s\|h_1-h\|_{C^s}.$$ Estimate this term using <a href="#corr:mass-inverse-two" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="corr:mass-inverse-two">[corr:mass-inverse-two]</a>. Estimate the remaining terms using the algebra property and <a href="#corr:mass-inverse-one" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="corr:mass-inverse-one">[corr:mass-inverse-one]</a>. On the closed $H^s$-ball of radius $r$, the Lipschitz constant of the right side of <a href="#corr:fixed-point" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="corr:fixed-point">[corr:fixed-point]</a> is at most $$C_s\|h_1-h\|_{C^s}
   +C_s\Lambda^{-1}\|V_0\|_{H^s}+C_s(r+r^2).$$ Choose a fixed small $r$ so that the last term is at most $1/4$ and Sobolev embedding makes $1+Y\ge1/2$ on this ball. Next choose $\delta$ so that the displayed Lipschitz constant is at most $1/2$ and the image of zero has norm at most $r/2$. The map is then a contraction of the ball into itself. Its fixed point obeys $$\|Y\|_{H^s}
 \le\tfrac12\|Y\|_{H^s}
                 +C_s\Lambda^{-1}\|V_0\|_{H^s},$$ which proves <a href="#corr:Z-smallness" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="corr:Z-smallness">[corr:Z-smallness]</a>. Finally the equation for $Z$, the smoothness of its coefficients, and elliptic regularity bootstrap $Z$ from $H^s$ to $H^{s+2}$, and then to all higher orders. The resulting real smooth solution remains positive. ◻

</details>

The factor $\Lambda$ in the nonlinear term is canceled by the first inverse bound, while the second inverse bound handles the change of the principal coefficients. These are estimates for the negative operator $\Delta_h-2\Lambda$; no high-energy nonresonance assumption is involved.

<a id="pm-h-42"></a>
###### Localization and exact determinant compatibility

<a id="corr:localized-data"></a>
<a id="corr:localized-flux"></a>
<a id="corr:defect-support"></a>
The global factor $Z$ need not equal one outside $P$. We localize it, keeping the resulting equation exact by recording its density. Choose a fixed $\chi\in C_c^\infty(U_4;[0,1])$ equal to one on a neighborhood of $\overline U_3$, and set $$\label{corr:localized-data}
 \widehat Z=1+\chi(Z-1),\qquad
 t=\frac v{\widehat Z},\qquad
 \rho=\widehat Z^2\left(1-\frac{V_0}{\Lambda_k}\right)
                +\frac{\widehat Z\Delta_{h_1}\widehat Z}{\Lambda_k}.$$ For sufficiently small $Z-1$ and $V_0$, both $\widehat Z$ and $\rho$ are positive. Direct differentiation gives $$\label{corr:localized-flux}
 \mathop{\mathrm{div}}_{h_1}(\widehat Z^2h_1^{-1}dt)
       =\widehat Z\Delta_{h_1}v-v\Delta_{h_1}\widehat Z
       =-\Lambda_k\rho t.$$ Where $\chi$ is identically one, <a href="#corr:Z-equation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="corr:Z-equation">[corr:Z-equation]</a> implies $\rho=\widehat Z^4$. Outside $\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}\chi$, the same equality holds because $V_0=0$ and $\widehat Z=1$. Thus $$\label{corr:defect-support}
 \mathop{\mathrm{supp}}(\rho-\widehat Z^4)
        \Subset \mathcal C:=U_4\setminus\overline U_2.$$ On this entire collar $h_1=g_0$ and $v=u_k^0$.

<a id="stmt-corr-localization"></a>
<div id="corr:localization" class="lemma">

<a id="corr:collar-input-estimate"></a>
<a id="corr:final-metric-estimate"></a>
**Lemma 38** (Determinant repair on the collar). *For the data <a href="#corr:localized-data" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="corr:localized-data">[corr:localized-data]</a>, suppose that $\widehat Z-1$ is sufficiently small in $C^1$ and $\rho>0$. Then an ordinary smooth metric $g_*$, equal to $g_0$ outside a compact subset of $U_4$, satisfies $(\Delta_{g_*}+\Lambda_k)t=0$. More quantitatively, if for some $N>0$, $$\label{corr:collar-input-estimate}
 \|h_1-h\|_{C^p}+\|V_0\|_{C^p}=O(k^{-N}),
 \qquad \|Z-1\|_{C^{p+2}}=O(k^{-N-2}),$$ then $$\label{corr:final-metric-estimate}
 \|g_*-h\|_{C^p}
             =O(k^{-N}+k^{p-N-2}).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-corr-localization"><summary>Proof of stmt-corr-localization</summary>

<a id="corr:normalized-collar-covector"></a>
*Proof.* Put $\phi=\theta+z$. Since $u_k^0=e^{kf_0}\cos(k\phi)$, the normalized covector on $\mathcal C$ is exactly $$\label{corr:normalized-collar-covector}
 \alpha:=\frac{dt}{ke^{kf_0}}
  =\widehat Z^{-1}
       \bigl(\cos(k\phi)\,df_0-\sin(k\phi)\,d\phi\bigr)
   -k^{-1}\cos(k\phi)\widehat Z^{-2}d\widehat Z.$$ On $\overline U_4$, the background covectors $df_0$ and $d\phi$ are orthogonal in $g_0$, with lengths bounded below. It follows that $|\alpha|_{h_1}\ge c>0$ for all sufficiently large $k$. In particular, <a id="corr:clean-gradient"></a>$$\label{corr:clean-gradient}
 |dt|_{h_1}\ge ck e^{kf_0}\quad\hbox{on }\mathcal C.$$ Let $$\Pi=I-\frac{\alpha^{\sharp_{h_1}}\otimes\alpha}
                       {|\alpha|_{h_1}^2}$$ be the $h_1$-orthogonal projection of the tangent bundle onto $\ker dt$ on $\mathcal C$. It has rank three and annihilates $\mathop{\mathrm{grad}}_{h_1}t$.

<a id="corr:flux-and-determinant"></a>
Define the positive scalar $$c_* =\left(\frac{\rho^2}{\widehat Z^8}\right)^{1/3}.$$ By <a href="#corr:defect-support" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="corr:defect-support">[corr:defect-support]</a>, $c_*-1$ has compact support in $\mathcal C$. Consequently $(c_*-1)\Pi$ extends smoothly by zero to $M$. Define a positive symmetric contravariant tensor and an ordinary metric by <a id="corr:determinant-tensor"></a>$$\label{corr:determinant-tensor}
 A'=\widehat Z^2\bigl(I+(c_*-1)\Pi\bigr)h_1^{-1},
 \qquad g_*^{-1}=\rho^{-1}A'.$$ Here the product containing $\Pi$ always means this smooth zero extension. In the normal direction to $\ker dt$, the endomorphism $A'h_1$ has eigenvalue $\widehat Z^2$; in the three tangent directions its eigenvalue is $\widehat Z^2c_*$. Hence $$\label{corr:flux-and-determinant}
 A'dt=\widehat Z^2h_1^{-1}dt,
 \qquad \det(A'h_1)=\widehat Z^8c_*^3=\rho^2.$$ Off the collar the same identities follow from $c_*=1$ and $\rho=\widehat Z^4$. Dimension four now gives $$\det(g_*^{-1}h_1)=\rho^{-4}\det(A'h_1)=\rho^{-2},
 \qquad d\mathrm{vol}_{g_*}=\rho\,d\mathrm{vol}_{h_1}.$$ It follows from <a href="#corr:localized-flux" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="corr:localized-flux">[corr:localized-flux]</a> and <a href="#corr:flux-and-determinant" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="corr:flux-and-determinant">[corr:flux-and-determinant]</a> that $$\Delta_{g_*}t
   =\rho^{-1}\mathop{\mathrm{div}}_{h_1}(A'dt)=-\Lambda_kt.$$ All changes are compactly supported in $U_4$.

It remains to verify the estimates without losing an exponential factor from $dt$. Under <a href="#corr:collar-input-estimate" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="corr:collar-input-estimate">[corr:collar-input-estimate]</a>, $\widehat Z\ge1/2$ and its derivatives through order $p+2$ are bounded independently of $k$. Formula <a href="#corr:normalized-collar-covector" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="corr:normalized-collar-covector">[corr:normalized-collar-covector]</a> gives $$|\partial^j\alpha|\le C_jk^j,
 \qquad |\partial^j\Pi|\le C_jk^j,
 \qquad 0\le j\le p.$$ The second estimate follows from the first, the fixed positive lower bound on $|\alpha|$, and the quotient rule. These powers are independent of the accuracy requested in the residual. On the region where $\chi\equiv1$, use $\rho=Z^4$. In the transition region use <a href="#corr:localized-data" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="corr:localized-data">[corr:localized-data]</a> with the fixed coefficients $h_1=g_0$, $V_0=0$, and outside $\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}\chi$ use $\rho=1$. Since $\Lambda_k$ is comparable to $k^2$, <a href="#corr:collar-input-estimate" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="corr:collar-input-estimate">[corr:collar-input-estimate]</a> therefore gives $$\|\rho-1\|_{C^p}+\|c_*-1\|_{C^p}=O(k^{-N-2}).$$ The product rule therefore yields $$\|(c_*-1)\Pi\|_{C^p}=O(k^{p-N-2}).$$ For the covariant metric, use the exact inverse supplied by $\Pi^2=\Pi$: $$g_*=\rho\widehat Z^{-2}h_1
                  \bigl(I+(c_*^{-1}-1)\Pi\bigr).$$ The scalar $c_*^{-1}-1$, like $c_*-1$, has $C^p$ norm $O(k^{-N-2})$. The product rule in this formula, together with $\|h_1-h\|_{C^p}=O(k^{-N})$, proves <a href="#corr:final-metric-estimate" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="corr:final-metric-estimate">[corr:final-metric-estimate]</a> for every $N>0$, including when its second term grows with $k$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-43"></a>
###### The finite order of choices

<details id="proof-stmt-corr-localization-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-corr-localization</summary>

*Proof of Lemma <a href="#corr:realization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="corr:realization">35</a>.* Here is one explicit order of choices. Given $p,L$, choose an even integer $s>p+4$, and set $$\begin{gathered}
 N=\lceil L\rceil+p+4,\qquad r=s+2,\qquad \ell=s+3,\\
 J=E_{s+2}+N,\qquad E_{s+2}=1000(s+4).
 \end{gathered}$$ Lemma <a href="#corr:weighted" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="corr:weighted">36</a> uses $u$ through order $s+4=\ell+1$ and $R$ through order $\ell$, and gives $$\|F\|_{C^{s+2}}+\|\vartheta\|_{C^{s+2}}=O(k^{-N}).$$ Equation <a href="#corr:potential-estimate" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="corr:potential-estimate">[corr:potential-estimate]</a> then gives $$\|h_1-h\|_{C^{s+2}}+\|w-1\|_{C^{s+2}}
                           +\|V_0\|_{H^s}=O(k^{-N}).$$ For large $k$, Lemma <a href="#corr:conformal" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="corr:conformal">37</a> applies with $\Lambda=\Lambda_k$. Its estimate and the embedding $H^s\hookrightarrow C^{p+2}$, valid because $s>p+4$ in dimension four, give $$\|Z-1\|_{C^{p+2}}
       \le C\|Z-1\|_{H^s}=O(k^{-N-2}).$$ The hypotheses of Lemma <a href="#corr:localization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="corr:localization">38</a> now hold. Since $N\ge L+p+4$, its conclusion implies $\|g_*-h\|_{C^p}=O(k^{-L})$. The eigenfunction is exactly $$t=\frac{w^{1/2}}{\widehat Z}\,u,$$ with a smooth positive multiplier equal to one off a compact subset of $U_4$. This proves every assertion.

All derivative orders and powers have been fixed before the wave construction is asked to provide <a href="#corr:derivatives" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="corr:derivatives">[corr:derivatives]</a>–<a href="#corr:residual" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="corr:residual">[corr:residual]</a>. The constants and the eventual lower threshold for $k$ may depend on that finite construction. Increasing its Taylor orders changes those constants, but neither the fixed noncancellation exponent nor the polynomial losses above. Thus only finitely many accuracy requirements are used at each realization step. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:spectral"></a>
##### Simplicity, persistence, and one fixed metric

The preceding construction produces an exact eigenfunction with a large sign score. We first make its eigenvalue simple while preserving that function exactly. The freedom required for this step is supplied by the three directions tangent to its level sets in an untouched part of $P$.

<a id="stmt-spec-simple"></a>
<div id="spec:simple" class="lemma">

**Lemma 39** (Simplicity preserving an eigenfunction). *Let $g$ be a smooth metric on $M$, and suppose $$-\Delta_g t=\Lambda_k t,\qquad k\ge1,$$ $$g=g_0,\quad t=u_k^0
 \quad\hbox{outside a compact subset of }P.$$ In every smooth-topology neighborhood of $g$ there is a smooth metric $\widetilde g$ for which $\Lambda_k$ is simple, with the same eigenfunction $t$. Moreover, $\widetilde g-g$ has compact support in $P$, and $d\operatorname{vol}_{\widetilde g}=d\operatorname{vol}_g$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-spec-simple"><summary>Proof of stmt-spec-simple</summary>

*Proof.* We shall use only perturbations in open subsets of $P$ where the metric and eigenfunction still agree with the background. Such a region remains after any finite number of compactly supported changes.

First, the squared gradient norm of the background varies along its level sets. More precisely, on every nonempty open subset of $P$ there is a smaller open subset on which $dt$ and $d|dt|_{g_0}^2$ are independent when $t=u_k^0$. Indeed, writing $\phi=\theta+z$, Equation <a href="#env:background-gradient-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="env:background-gradient-identity">[env:background-gradient-identity]</a> gives $$|du_k^0|_{g_0}^2
 =k^2\bigl(H_k(p)-2(u_k^0)^2\bigr),\qquad
 H_k(p)=\sin^{2k}p+\sin^{2k-2}p.$$ The first summand in $H_k$ has strictly positive derivative on $0<p<\pi/2$; the second is increasing for $k>1$ and constant for $k=1$. Thus $H_k'(p)>0$ for every $k\ge1$. Wherever $\sin(k\phi)\ne0$, the covectors $du_k^0$ and $dp$ are independent, so $$d|du_k^0|_{g_0}^2\wedge du_k^0
 =k^2 H_k'(p)\,dp\wedge du_k^0\ne0.$$ The excluded set has empty interior.

<a id="spec:local-dependence"></a>
Let $E=\ker(-\Delta_g-\Lambda_k)$ have dimension $m>1$, and choose a real $t_2\in E$ independent of $t$. Take an open background region $V\Subset P$ on which $dt$ and $d|dt|_g^2$ are independent. We claim that the tangential component of $\mathop{\mathrm{grad}}_g t_2$ along a level set of $t$ is nonzero somewhere in $V$. Otherwise $dt_2$ vanishes on $\ker dt$ throughout $V$. Since $dt\ne0$, submersion coordinates on a smaller product neighborhood give $t_2=F(t)$ for a smooth one-variable function $F$. The two eigenfunction equations imply $$F''(t)|dt|_g^2=\Lambda_k\bigl(tF'(t)-F(t)\bigr).
 \label{spec:local-dependence}$$ On a still smaller neighborhood there is a smooth vector field $X$ with $Xt=0$ and $X(|dt|_g^2)\ne0$. Differentiating <a href="#spec:local-dependence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:local-dependence">[spec:local-dependence]</a> along $X$ gives $F''(t)=0$ there. The image of this neighborhood under the submersion $t$ contains an open interval; hence $F(r)=ar+b$ on that interval. Substitution into <a href="#spec:local-dependence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:local-dependence">[spec:local-dependence]</a>, using $\Lambda_k>0$, forces $b=0$. Consequently $t_2-at$ vanishes on a nonempty open set.
